The Japanese idiom is more physical and embodied, closer to "walk tall" — a quiet dignity rather than pride. Given Cloud's usual emotional restraint, the physical imagery is characteristically understated.

    6. Barret's inner monologue — Holy is everyone's prayer

    Literal "Holy is the prayer of AVALANCHE… of Marlene and Dyne… the prayer of the people of this Planet!"
    Japanese 「ホーリーはアバランチの……マリンやダインの……この星の人間のいのりだ！」
    English "Holy is the prayer of AVALANCHE… / Of Marlene and Dyne… / And everyone on the Planet!"

    The English says "everyone on the Planet," which is slightly abstract. The Japanese says 「この星の人間」 — literally "the humans of this Planet." The specificity of 人間 (human beings, people) is meaningful: it centers Holy as a prayer of human life, not of all life or the Planet itself. This matters because Holy is shortly about to conflict with the Lifestream, which represents all life. The Japanese quietly holds the human-vs-Planet tension that the ending crystallizes.

    7. Sephiroth's soliloquy — "everything will begin anew with me"

    Literal "And from this very me, everything begins!!"
    Japanese 「そしてこの私からすべてがはじまる！！」
    English "Now everything / will begin anew…with me!!"

    The Japanese 「この私から」 — "from this very me" or "from this self of mine" — is extraordinarily self-focused. The emphasis on 「この」 (this, this very) and the formal 「私」 make it almost narcissistic in a clinical sense. The English "with me" is collaborative-sounding by comparison. Sephiroth is not proposing to restart things in partnership with himself; he is declaring himself the sole origin point of all future existence.

    8. Vincent's awakening — "frozen in time"

    Literal "The time of the me that froze in that moment — now, once again, it begins to move…!"
    Japanese 「あの時に凍りついた私の時間が、今ふたたび動きはじめる……！」
    English "I was frozen in time… / But now I feel as though my time / is finally beginning!"

    The Japanese is a single flowing sentence where Vincent's frozen past-self and his present-self are grammatically unified — 「あの時に凍りついた私の時間」, "the time of me who froze at that time." The English splits it into a before/after structure that makes the transformation more explicit but loses the sense that the same person's suspended clock is now resuming. The Japanese phrasing is more elegiac and less declarative.

    9. CAIT SITH — "We noticed a little late" — guilt vs. error

    Literal "Perhaps it was a little too late when we noticed the mistake…"
    Japanese 「まちがいにきづくんがちぃ～と遅かったんかもしれん……」
    English "We noticed a little late the trouble we were getting in…"

    The Japanese says 「まちがい」 — mistake, error. The English renders it as "the trouble we were getting in," which frames it as a situational problem you stumble into. The Japanese frames it as a moral and cognitive failure: we made a mistake and noticed it too late. Cait Sith, who spent much of the game as a spy and betrayer, gives this line a confessional weight in Japanese that the English softens into general misfortune.

    10. Cloud's "OK, I'm going alone" — mistranslated as the opposite

    Literal "I'll fight here too."
    Japanese 「俺もここで戦う」
    English "OK, I'm going alone."

    This is a direct reversal of meaning. The English has Cloud accepting that he'll push on by himself. The Japanese has Cloud saying he wants to stay and fight alongside his friends — which is why Barret immediately calls him an idiot and overrules him. The exchange only makes sense in Japanese: Cloud tries to be loyal, Barret forces him forward. In English, Cloud seems to misunderstand or ignore the instruction entirely, making Barret's response incoherent.

    11. Yuffie's battle cry — "I don't like it!!"

    Literal "I can't take this anymore — !!"
    Japanese 「もうイヤ～ッ！！」
    English "I don't like it!!"

    「もうイヤ」 is a very Japanese exclamation of being completely fed up or at one's limit — closer to "I'm done with this!" or "I can't stand it anymore!" than a measured preference. In context, every party member gets a defiant war cry at this moment; Yuffie's is deliberately anti-heroic. The English captures the deflation but "I don't like it" sounds too mild and conversational, losing the comedic over-the-top exhaustion of the Japanese.

    12. Red XIII's final vow — "the Planet's life" vs. "the Lifestream"

    Literal "The Lifestream… the life of the Planet — I will not let it wither!!"
    Japanese 「ライフストリームを……星の命を、かれさせはしない！！」
    English "I won't let the Lifestream… / or the Planet wither away!!"

    The English combines "Lifestream" and "the Planet" as two things. The Japanese uses 「星の命」 — "the life of the Planet" — as the second term, making the Lifestream and the Planet's life parallel: Red XIII is vowing to protect the living force of the Planet itself, not just the Planet as a physical body. 「かれさせはしない」 (will not let wither/dry up) is a word used for plants dying — entirely appropriate for the Planet as a living organism, and for Red XIII specifically who has the deepest ecological consciousness.

    13. The ending — "An answer from the planet… the Promised Land"

    Literal "An answer from the Planet… the Promised Land…"
    Japanese 「星からの容え….約束の地….」
    English "An answer from the planet… / the Promised Land…"

    The Japanese uses 「容え」, an unusual or archaic rendering of 答え (answer/response), suggesting something more like a "response" or "acceptance" from the Planet rather than a simple answer. More critically, this line has no clear speaker assigned — it floats between characters (possibly Aeris, possibly the Planet itself). The English renders it identically, but the ambiguity of who is speaking is the point: the Promised Land is being offered as an answer without a clearly human source.

    14. "I think I can meet her … there"

    Literal "Over there… I think I can meet [her]."
    Japanese 「そこで….会えると思うんだ」
    English "I think I can meet her … there"

    The Japanese drops the pronoun entirely — 「会えると思うんだ」 has no explicit "her." Japanese allows subject/object omission, so the referent is implied from context (Aeris). The English translator added "her" to clarify, which is standard — but the deliberate vagueness in Japanese creates a moment where the identity of the person being met is briefly suspended, a small structural echo of Aeris's ambiguous fate. The line is also notably casual (「思うんだ」 is soft, uncertain), resisting the triumphalism the English might suggest.

    15. "Shit!! / The flower girl?" — the ending's tonal collision

    Literal "Damn it all!!" / "The flower-selling big sister?"
    Japanese 「ちくしょー！！」 / 「お花のおねえちゃん？」
    English "Shit!!" / "The flower girl?"

    「お花のおねえちゃん」 is a child's way of referring to Aeris — literally "the older sister who sells flowers." The speaker is almost certainly Marlene. The English "flower girl" is accurate as a gloss but loses the precise register: in Japanese it is unambiguously a small child's voice, tender and slightly naive. The phrase confirms that the ending is seen partly through a child's eyes, which sharpens the emotional contrast with the apocalyptic imagery surrounding it. "Flower girl" is just a job description; 「お花のおねえちゃん」 is a relationship.
